LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE,

I saw the other day a hat which a friend of mine thought looked very charming, although personally I had an impression that it was rather heavily trimmed. However, I must confess chat after a closer scrutiny I was bound to admit that the hat looked well. I will let you judge tut- yourself, and so I will try and very briefly describe it. It was a plain black satin tilted slightly for. ward, showing, however, the wearer's pretty curls of hair which extended along the side of the head. Around and above the brim was placed some beautiful blue silk, made up into 9. Low at the back and gradually thinning towards the front until it became almost of the width of one inch ribbon. On the left hand side of the hat were bows of black material which had an edging of sable fur. At the side there was a very pretty paste ornament with four large imitation pearls. The fur gave the hat a seasonable appearance, although as I have said my first impression of it was that it looked heavy. Perhaps you will think differently to me.

At the opera the other evening my friend the Duchess of -- was wearing a beautiful evening dress. As you will know, she has recently lost a distant relation, and on that account she appeared in a somewhat sombre although smart dress. The bodice took my fancy because it fitted her splen. didly. As I have already told you the bodice was of black, the material being satin. This was made on a pale bodice for evening wear, in pink lining, the top of which was veiled in folds of delicate pink chiffon, which crossed the short fiat sleeve ol pink silk lining, and gave the effect of folds crow ing the arm and pinned down at the sides with a single rose. At the top of the bodice was a band of black silk, which looked very smart and gave quite a rich appearance to the dress. Besidea, it looked very neat—in fact I could find nothing tc complain of in the arrangement as a whole.

You will be pleased to hear that sleeves in even. ing dresses as in ball gowns have grown smaller although they had a tendency to enlargement at the shoulders. The other day at an evening party I saw a fine dress which I thought worthy of tak- ing a note of so as to describe it to you. The bodice was very slightly made of blue de ciel plan de soie with full vest of ivory chiffon and the decolletage surrounded by a garland of violets and foliage. The revers were edged with narrow bonillonaees of chiffon, and the long sleeves were of ruffled chiffon, mounted on a fitted lining of net. The costume showed off to great advantage, whilst the wearer was rendering a song which she sang charmingly.

The favourite blue of the moment is called pastel blue, and has a considerable dash of grey in it that seems to be more on the surface than in the actual fabric.

In an up-to-date dress the upper skirt is plain, but edged with narrow silver passementerie, which is also carried up the bodice as far as the revers. The under-skirt is stamped out in the darker blue, and lined with the lighter one. The vest is folded blue silk and has a &Liu of rich guipure laid over it, and tucked in under the velvet revere which are of the deeper shade, and look even darker than they are on account of the difference of material. The waistband and wired knot and the inside of the turnover collar are of the same. The sleeves are edged with silver passementerie, and have a narrow quilling of blue gauze ribbon inside them. I saw the other day a lady whose hair was arranged in a smart coiffure. The front is composed of natur ally curly hair, with long wavy hair at- tached to comb over towards the coil. These fringes are invaluable to ladies with thin hair, as they never come out of curl or wave with damp or heat, and save all the trouble of waving arti- ficially the hair which is growing on the head. For this reason they are also much worn by people who have thick hair, as they save a great deal of time. The coils to complete the coiffures can be made in various styles, all of them have fashionable slides, in plain tortoiseshell and handsomely jewelled. Wishing you all the compliments of the season, Your loving. JENNIF.

ACCIDENT ON TRW CHESHIRE LINES RAILWAY. On Ftiday night last on the arrival of the 11-30 train from Liverpool to London at Warrington one of the carriage doors was found to be open. The unconscious body of a man was found about mid- night lying oa the Cheshire Lines Railway about 60 yards north of Rough Green Station. Mr. Robinson, stationmaster at Rough Green, was com- municated with, and the body was removed to the surgery of Dr. Mercer, Hough Green, who found that the man's skull was fractured, and that he was suffering from alicussion. From papers found in his possession he was identified as a farm labourer named Murray, of Upton, Widnes, and be was removed thither. He still remained in an unconscious condition on Tuesday night. It is supposed that the man travelled by the train men- tioned, and that on passing Hough Green, where the train slowed down, he jumped out.

Christmas at the Schloss, Cumberland, Graiinden,was very quietly spent, The Court mourning for the late Queen of Denmark and the Empress of Austria, has not yet been lightened, high black stuff gowns, trimmed profusely with crape, and long crape veils, being worn indoors as well as out. The aged King of Denmark who is still with his youngest daukhter, has borne up won- derfully, but it is no secret that the death of the Queen has struck a blow from which he will never wholly re- cover. The Duke and Duchess of Cumberland will in all probability, proceed to Vienna shortly, and the King of Denmark is expectf dto arrive in this country on a visit to the Queen and the Prince and Princess of Wales. Chatsworth, where the Duke and Duchess of Devon- shire are entertaining right royally, is situated on a gentle rise near the base of a finely wooded hill, the Derwenc spanned by a handsome stone bridge, flowing past its principal front. The n ansion forms a square,of 190 feet, enclosing a spacioias court, with a fountain in the centre. It has a flat roof, surrounded by balustrades and is decorated with lonic columns. At the principal entrance a grand flight of steps leads to a terrace ex- tending the length of the building. The grand hall and state apartments are very imposing, the living rooms comfortable, as well as handsome. There is a wonder- ful collection of pictures at Chatsworth, and also a hall full of statuary, and artich s of vertu. The present edifice stands on the site of the mansion built by Sir William Cavendish in the sixteenth century, in which Mary Queen of Scots was imprisoned thirteen years. It was comple'ed in 1706, but a wing and other additions have been made to it in the present century. Chatsworth was but little fr quented by the late Duke, who much preferred Volker, his Lancashire home. This is scarcely to be marvelled at, as. despite its palatial attributes, Chatsworth used to be singularly deficient in very ordinary requirements. For instance, there were no blinds to the bedroom windows, and not any of the apartments possessed bell pulls. Now however all de- fects have been made pod, and comfort reigns sup- reme. _ .

Perhaps no one receives more presents at this particu- lar season than the Duchess of Devonshire. Her gifts have been endless, and came from Royalty downwards. The giving of Christmas presents is a graceful way of acknowledging, if not returning social oblieat ons,but— they are becoming som ,c,i,hat a tax. As in the similar case of wedding presents, they are influenced by com- petition, and since wealthy folks are in the habit of giving jewels, or jewelled knick-knacks, poorer indivi- duals seem foolishly to think they are obliged to do the same. _ .

In many Churches, ctrol singing took the piece of a sermon on the afternoon of Chrislmse Day, and in almost all a few carols were sung during, or after one of the services. The carol " Christiana Awake," which has become one of the most widely sung of all Christmas hymns, was written by Dr. John By om, and is really, of much higher merit than the generality of carols. The carol was undoubtedly originally like the Greek chorus, a song and dance combine I, indeed the Welsh word carol still maintains the simple meaning of " dance " alone. The earliest printed colt, ction of English Christmas carols was math by Wyenkvn de Words in 1521, and it would seem oe'd that they sit mid be mostly of a convivial character did we not know the passion in medimval times for mixing the sacred with the secular, or as Shakspeire says "singing psalms to Lorepipes." The Appledore railway accident aids yet another to the list of Christmas railway disasters, which are legion, many of them, owing to the exceptionally heavy traffic having proved unusually fatal. Perhaps thy best remembered is the Tay Bridge disaster, of 1879, by which seventy ot eighty people lost their lives. Among other bad Christmas traia smashes were the Shipton, 1874 ; when some forty people lost their lives ; that near Heathfield, 1870, by which more than a score of people were injured, and six killed outright ; the one at Arlsey siding, near Hitchen, 1876; and the awful collision between two express trains of the New York Central Railway, late on Christmas Eve, 1891. To the British Navy, December 25th, has already been a fatal day, though perhaps the most terrible of all these Christmas ocean tragedies was that whlch took place on the night of December 24th, 1811, when the St. George, the Defence, and the Hero, werJ wree ked off the Coast of Julland. There perished on this occa- sion, the Admiral Reynolds, and more than 2,000 of his officers and men. Among more recent Christmas wrecks, there stands out pre-eminett, the loss of the Blervie Castle, which fceandered ha the Channel with all hands ; that of the Ambassador, sunk in the Indian Ocean through a collision ; and the destruction, by fire, of the Shanghai in the China Seas, when nearly 300 persons perished. Miss Mary Kingsley intends to get forth again shortly on another West African Exped tion. There is no need to wonder where she gets her love of adventure, and her literary style. Her father was as enthusiastic in it as her uncle Charles. Miss Kingsley began her explora- tions seven years ago, on the North Coast ef Africa, and it was net till the following year that she gave her- self up to exploring the West Coast, wth a special in- tention of studying the idol-worship of the various tribes. That year was spent amongst the districts which lie be- tween the Congo River and Simla Lisette, In 1894 Miss Kingsley undertook &second expedition to these regions, and devoted herself to the study of the savages in and around Ashantee.

Miss Kingsley lays down three rules as being especially necessary for all who would wish to accomplish SUCCOV- fuIIy any travels or explorations in such regions as West Africa. They are patience, perseverance, and courage. Possessed of a sound and fairly vigorous constitution, she has been able to ward off so fa r,the insidious attacks f African fever, to which every dweller in those regions is s'o subject. She has never yet had an attack at all,an item very few African explorers can boast of. Miss Kingsley's new book, which will soon appear now, will it seems be quite as largess her last great volume on her travels. .

Large hips are slowly but surely disappearing, a state of affairs to rejoice over, as the mode was not a pretty or graceful one. What the unfortunate individual will do whose rotundity is natural I know not but these who have been accustomed to pad are being unstuffed as ra- pidly as possible. All house gowns are made long bath in the front and at the sides, and a small train appears in almost all instances. Skirts for street wear are fairly short, another inch or so less in length however would be better, and should be insisted upon by those who study their health. Gowns of real antique lace, attributed variously to Marie Antoinette, or the Empress Josephine, are p'eati- ful this winter, and if all one meets are genuine,one can only Some to the conclusion, that these two illustrous women must have done little else except present them- selves with new lace frocks. For young girls pretty dresses, mousseino de soie, tulle chiffon, and gauze, re- main the favourite fabrics. These dainty cog ctions are very elaborately ruched and ruffled, either with the same material as that of which the frock is built,er with narrow gauze or velvet ribbons.

_ Babies have long ceased to have a monopoly of the little narrow ribbons, and they are now applied to the garments of the most mature. They heed flounces, or edge them, they appear close'y gathered on skits, to simulate the polannaise, or other form of trimming, on blouse and bodices of all descriptions, they abound while in the millenary world they are more sought after than ever I saw the other day,two delightful bonnets built for an elderly dame, and both boasted strings made of baby ribbon, fortified by strips of velvet. The effect when tied in a fascineing bow, was very light and pretty. SECRET OF MARK PEPYS.

BY FRED J. PROCTOR, Author of "Timothy Twill's Secret," "Jean

CHAPTER XXVII. MARK VERSUS MIRO.

In retracing my steps to Stony Hollow I cotmel over our conversation, probed it for motives, examined it as a pilot would the storm-beaten shore, wondering whether, by remaining at anchor, the good ship would be driven on the quicksands, or if, in sailing forward, she would become a wreck on some desolate spot. That night sleep was a devoted nurse, and before daybreak hunger added a delicious sauce to my fare. I was anxious to reach home and apprise Mark of his danger. On crossing the glacier I noticed that the stakes driven into the ice had travelled down towards the valley, a little distance beyond those stuck into the hard snow lodged between the crags. Thus the mighty ice-field moved at the rate of about three-quarters of an inch every twenty-four hours. Now the third day of my journey brought a dis- agreeable surprise in the shape of a messenger with bad tidings. The pard had started with us from Puke's Delight after the robbery of Mark's treasures ; he had fought with heroic determination at San Leno, and been true as steel throughout our j ourneyings. "Darn glad to see you well, Sandy," said he, shaking my hand heartily, "fer I calkilate times hey gone hard wi' us in camp." I opened my eyes and stammered: "Gang hard? What's up? ' "I'm gosh darned ef thet rascal Muro, him ez set a price on our boss's head," responded the man with a good round backwoods' ' oath or two, "ain't pounced on our camp. He thought hisself a clever coon, but I guess he'll swing in the noose he made fer our gaffer. He flung a boomerang at Mark's head, an it slung back an' whacked his own."

"How are Mark an' his wife?" "The boss is perky, but his pooty missis frets. Well, you know, Sandy, grub'B. scarce, an' I reckon a woman wi' an empty larder can't shew off her cookin'. Mebbe she'll buck up now the scoundrel ez chucked up the sponge. The tussle was suthin' hot, I tell you. At one time it looked ten to one on ,Muro, but Mark fought like a dozen men rolled into one—no covortin' about permialcus like, but at close quarters, jest ez he done at San Leno. The Spaniard crushed the little finger of Mark's left hand; God knows how 'twas done! Say, Sandy, how hey ye fared ? " "Never mind me," said I impatiently, "tell me all aboot the attack. Can't ye see I'm burning t' learn."

"No hurry, Sandy, now the cussed snake's ez good ez dead. I've come to fetch ye to his roasting. Jest don't worrit me while I light my pipe. Mud yer pouch, mate, my baccy's well- nigh run out." I struck a lucifer, and when his pipe was lighted he told me the sten), between his puffs. Tantalis- ing as this was, yet I dare not hurry him. "I got through wi'out a scratch. Thar was no time fer crookin' in the whiskey, ef thar'd been any left. It was like this 'ere. Pecos got wind o sutlain' wrong from some gels as come swaggerin' about the camp the day a'ter ye left. Pecos jest got canoodlin' round them gels thet much ez made one on 'ens blow the gaff. Puttin' two an' two together Mark guessed mischief was brewin' fer thet night. Shall we scuttle the ship, mates, an' take to the open ? ' asked the boss. We preferred to fight rather than skiddadle. To run sway would hov' been a sorter kind of shewin' the white feather."

"What, was Olive's advice ? " "Well, I reckon she entered into the thing like a brave woman," said my companion with ran. "She bartered her beads an' trinkets wi the Indian women fer good wholesome food, Bich ea a feller kin fight on, an' we all made a darned good meal. Then at dusk we let our fires smoulder, an' after dark we filed off on our ponies, one by one, by the gully leadin' west-nor'-west from Stony Hollow. This feint was suggested by Olive. I guess it was done jest about smart—a sorter kind o strategy thet gives men confidence. Well, twat grippin' our shootin'-irons an' keepin' a smart look-out two hours passed wi'out a move from the enemy." My companion stopped short and thoughtfully drew the palm of his grimy hand down his long unkempt hair, then, remembering the successive events of his narrative, he pinched his beard, poked the tobacco down in his pipe, and continued in his own peculiar style. "The Matherkites reckoned ez how we laid asleep, an' came ridin' route an' round, a score or more, the chief Moro Taisself ridin' forrards wi' care, an' then, no doubt, orderin"em to dis- mount. Two of 'em stoppeek mindin' tiler ponies, which Mark determined he'dicut off, an' I was one to tackle this job. Olive carried two loaded pistols, an' made good work wi' 'em. Well, the enemy crawled like insects towards the corral, an' our boys spread round closin"em in by degrees. Mark an' four of our lads hid away near the line of retreat for ther ponies. I kin tell ye, Sandy, we meant fight. Though all of us pitied Mark's wife, ez ain't thet robust jest now." "It was hardly the place fer a wuman in deeli- cate health," said I, by way of relieving the monotony of his nasal twang. "But she bore up suthin' powerful, an' when the devils heaped dry wood round her cabin an' set fire to it, Mark was furious, for he shuddered on guessin' ther fate, ef the couple had been inside thet cabin."

"What was yer signal to attack?" "Mark's whistle, an' when its shrill notes give us the chance we made them cussed Indians howl wi' rage. Our first volley picked off half a dozen. I shot one hostler through the heart, but my mate missed fire an' hit a pony. T'other feller scampered off, leavin' the ponies to the two of us; an', lest they should make fer the fire, we led 'em away." "A capital dodge," said I, "fer the enemy found their retreat cut off." _

"Yah ! " responded my companion with a broad grin. "We dished the blackguards, an' our lads closed in. I bad my work cut out to tether the ponies, an' when I did rush forrards to lend a hand the enemy as didn't lay welterin' in ther blood had took to ther heels. An' I guess they're done fer. But I see we've come to Stony Hollow, so let Mark tell the rest."

The camp reached, I gave my experiences, and Mark described the fight in detail. He was much bruised, and his wounded finger painful. The danger faced by Olive had won general admiration, bitt they declared Mark's escape from death was miraculous. The enemy was unable to reload after two of our lads had dropped, for our leader and the men rushed on them and engaged at close quarters. The ground about the burning cabin was soaked with blood, and not a square yard but what shewed evidences of a fearful struggle. Mark and Muro had expended their shots, and while the former was heeling the brigand rushed upon him. Mark, taken unawares, whisked out his long hunting knife, and was defending himself when one of our lads shot Muro through the right shoulder. Then the brigand seized his knife with the left hand, but Mark sprang to his feet and felled him to the ground with the butt end of his musket. There Muro lay senseless, and the others, seeing their chief fallen, made good their escape. One huge redskin was shot in the back by Olive, his body falling with a thud upon a -granite pavement, where he was found in a heap after the melee. '

Altogether thirteen hostile redskins lay dead, for no quarter was asked, and the slightly wounded were despatched at once, lest they should cunningly stab or shoot our men. This precaution was absolutely necessary. Pecos had a flesh wound in the thigh, our Indian guide had a nasty wound on the skull, and most of the others were more or less brnised or slightly wounded. At the first breathing time our lads clamoured for Muro to be hanged, but Mark stoutly resisted. "It looks too much like murder, lads," argued our chief, "and besides, we must study my wife's health." "The deuce take sentiment!" fiercely cried one. "I reckon Muro never spared us!" "Let us carry the rascal beyond Stony Hollow and burn him! demanded another, who pace.: around the prostrate Spaniard in a threateninE attitude, swearing at him as fast as his tongue could go. "Hold!" exclaimed Mark. "Grant me his lift till daylight. Sandy will soon be here, let him decide:"

" Wire him time for repentance," urged Olive. "He may confess much." "Bahl " answered the first objector. "The lamb pleads for the wolf, and he'll turn and rend her to p i eces. " "Stay! You are all mad with rage," shouted Mark, 'and our wounds smart. Let him live suffi- ciently long to feel his degradation. If, in our cooler moments, we say a lingering death is his just punishment I will let you have your own way. If you kill him outright now, you will defeat my aim. He may confess to-morrow in the hope of, saving his 'neck at he expense of somebody else." Mark advanced this argument to ease his con- science of the burden of summarily hanging a man. Torture was foreign to his purpose, though the hint satisfied the lads, and the notorious brigand was left in the hands of a captain who often shelved more good-nature than wisdom. Jip shared the honour of victory, for he kept at bay a hostile Indian who crept up the hill with the intention of cutting the ponies loose. The redskin was discovered insensible not twenty yards from them, the marks of teeth in his throat, and his arms and breast torn. jip was a bit mauled, but the noble animal forgot his pain in the excitement of triumph. Apart from wounds, perhaps the one who longest felt the ill effects of the dastardly surprise was Mark's wife • for when I entered the camp she was still suffering from hysteria and nervous prostration. After Mark had given me his modest version of the attack, I asked the whereabouts of Muro. "Come and see," said he, painfully limping on ahead.

"Ony bones broken, Mark ? " "No, Sandy; simply stiff. Joints Want oiling. Muro is bound within the corral. Step inside." .1 did so, and started with surprise when I saw the brigand's deplorable condition. He lay flat on his back on a bed of dried leaves and moss, his shock head resting on a saddle, and a blanket over his body. Muro turned his bloodshot eyes away from us—not from fear, but for very shame. "He may thank his lucky stars that Olive was here," observed Mark after we left. "He hasn't spoken a sentence since the attack. A sullen in- difference as to his fate seems to have seized him."

"Mere bravado," I suggested. "No; for on regaining consciousness he expected me to revenge myself. I must have knocked him half silly with the butt end of my gun." "Who bound up his wounded shoulder ? " ' "My wife. Why do you stare ? " ,"Well," I answered in an incredulous tone, "this is a queer retaliation. I always thought the parable o' the Good Samaritan a myth. Muro not only refused to have dealings wi' ye, but tried to murder ye; an', moreover, set a price upon yer heid. An' ye allow Olive to nurse him! All honourable warfare demands that a fellow like Muro should be immediately shot. Yers is the act o' a Christian gaen aff his heid! Ye smoke yer pipe upon a barrel o' gunpowder! " "Look here, Sandy; I don't believe half the puffs' about people's goodness; and Muro is like Satan, often dressed as a black scarecrow to frighten timid people." "Then if he is incapablg o' doing meeschief, why tie his legs to a stake? "Only as a poulterer ties the legs of a fowl—to prevent escape; only as a matter of precaution, and to satisfy the lads. He thought I should have cut his throat, or blown out his brains. When Olive saw him his eyeballs seemed to start from their sockets. His condition was a sickening sight, and you could see by his face how a calf whitens as the blood flows. My wife moistened his lips—oh! don't be alarmed, Sandy—and he closed his-eyes with a moan of anguish, as though he suffered untold mental agony. 'Then the un- expected came: hot tears welfed into his weary 'yes, and forced their way down his cheeks. Sandy, this man has led a dual life: fierce as a brigand, and perhaps not base as a citizen." "The Saracen mothers frightened their children hy making a bogey o' Cceur de lion. Mebbe Muro has been similarly misrepresented. If so, why his hatred o'ye ? " "Perhaps a third party has whispered that I seek his death, for the sake of the reward said to be offered by the Government." " Reward! ' I exclaimed with a cynical smile. "A mere subterfuge—Mexican perfidy Muro is either dumb or perverse." "The lads certainly give some common-sense reasons why he should be hanged." "Necessity in this case demands muir than mere rule o' thumb discipline," I urged, to feel the pulse of Mark's temper. "Muro is like Bona- parte: while he lives, nabody is safe. In sickness he's a burden; in 'health a menace. If a com- mander's forgi'eness endangers his followers, he is responsible fer their sufferings." "Then have his grave dug a hundred paces from the Union Jack, due north," grimly orderedi Mark. "He shall have a fair trial first, though," he , added, after a moment's thought; "and if he can plead extenuating circumstances, he shall be shot like a soldier, and buried decently." There was a curious hesitancy in Mark's manner, as though extraneous circumstances or possiblk_ contingences affected his decision, so I said: ' "What if Muro shews base ingratitude ? " "Then," he indignantly replied, his'voice laden with sUperabundant scorn, "hang the villain to the tree nearest his grave; let his flesh be food for vultures, and his skeleton a scarecrow to frighten his Indian allies! But mind, Sandy, this must be kept from Olive." 'He sighed, and sharply drew his hand across his aching forehead. What mental agony this decision cost him was studiously kept from all of us; nor for sometime was I even to guess why he faltered. "Mark, is that yer final decision?" "I have no alternative. He shall die—that is final. See to it at once, and let me get it off my. 'mind. When a man has a disagreeable duty to perform he wants to get shut of it as soon as possible. God knows, it is a wretched business." The boys were willing gravediggers, for Mure was detested by most Europeans; and, it must be owned, we had good cause for our hatred. Within a week we had struck camp; half going ahead with Olive about nine o'clock in the morning, and the rest remaining for the execution. Everything was done decently and in order. It was a horrible ordeal to bring the culprit forth. Yet the rascal did not behave as a coward, for he treated his approaching end with stolid indifference, and walked between two of our men with head erect and step as firm as his weak condition permitted. When he came to the place of execution, and saw his grave dug, a visible shudder passed through his body and his lips turned white; but it was only a momentary weakness. . .

Mark's face shewed the terrible effort this reprisal cost him, but he observed with a stern and solemn voice: "You are here, Miro; to suffer the extreme penalty for your base designs against my life, and for the murder of my men. Can you give any just cause why this punishment should not be carried out? Speak, now, for it is your last opportunity." Muro cast his eyes upward, and shook his head. Mark, remained silent; it was an extremely painful moment.

"Answer me, Muro. Why have you dogged my footsteps and sought to kill me ? " But the con- demned Spaniard spake never a word, and when a lad threw the noose about his neck he neither flinched nor moaned. "The fellow is no coward," muttered Mark to me. "I likg him for that." Then a woman's scream rang through the woods, and arrested our attention. Peering along a small colonnade, I saw the figure of Lucia Diaz. This denouement was so unexpected, that the Sefiorita's appearance almost took Mark's breatliaway ; in fact, Muro himself staggered a pace, ominously in the direction of his newly-dug grave. The situa- tion, so tragic in significance, acted like. a spell upon the lads. The hangmen, who simply waited the signal to hoist, now involuntarily let the rope go slack. On came Lucia, panting with excite- ment, and determined by screams and gestures to stay the hands of the executioners. Mark beckoned vine to accompany him, and then folding his arms he walked forward so that our conversation should be out of earshot. "Rae patience, lads," I said in an undertone, for our men were beginning to grumble; "his death will be on yer hands, unless ye wait till the signal is gi en." Lucia was about to assume a role that required great resource—an unsavoury task which she undertook without mincing matters. "Spare Muro's life," she cried "till I explain all!" . _

She gasped for breath—Panted like a deer hard pressed in the chase, and leaning against a tree would have fallen but for my support. There were "dark rings round her eyes, the veins of her' fore- head stood out like knotted whipcord; and from her face and lips every tinge of colour had fled. Just then I looked into Mark's face, and instantly saw his struggle for composure. Was there noi some excuse for his slight trepidation, for had he not adored Lucia; and was not this very woman now pleading for a rival's life?

CHAPTER XXVIII. FOR THE DEFENCE,

a. come," she began, after fighting down a sickly feeling which was worse than complete fainting, "at considerable inconvenience and with some amount of regret, to ask a• favour of you. Thank God I am in time."

Mark bowed stiffly; and Lucia paused for breath.

"ALM° is but guilty of attacking what he con- sidered a hostile foe. When I knelt beside the graves of your men at Sari Leno I swore to dis- cover the thief who stole your treasures at Puke's Delight. Muro was not that man." "Nonsense!" responded Mark somewhat im- patiently; "you are fooling us. The schooner was his; besides, he murdered the half-bred labourer who defended Pecos."

"You have no proof of Muro's connivance in the robbery or murder. Again, he was not the only Zuropean directing the Beige of San Leno." "Why attack me so persistently ? " "His mind was poisoned against you." "Humbug!" irritably retorted Mark.. "Next you will want me to believe that Muro was ignorant of the stealing of Kana's dead ancestors." Lucia haughtily drew herself up at full length, replying: "Muro was absolutely ignorant of that desecration. That was Goya's revenge; Planned and carried out by him as a reprisal against you. Olive's captors seized her plan of the caves, and Goya managed to obtain it. I would not jeopardise you, Mark; but I tell you I hated Olive!" "What a spitfire 1" thought I, as these words were hissed between her pearly teeth. Yet another phase of this woman's character was revealed. She inter- preted the look upon my face as condemnation; and her countenance grew anxious, as her pleading was more skilfully importunate. _ _ _

Mark observed severely: "Olive had done you no wrong." "I was anxious for you to save her life when she was a captive and doomed to death, and she repaid my kindness by winning your love. That I was careful for your welfare, you may possess proof in your own handwriting. I placed my jewelled cross —that which I carried from Yerba Buena—upon the mantelpiece in Sandy's room. Upon it I scratched a word or two with this hairpin." She touched a miniature dagger in her hair, adding: "I presume you saw the cross, and read my words " Mark bowed, but his features did not relax one iota.

"The hest proof of my truthfulness is this," urged Lucia, now speaking with more confidence. "Had Muro known the outward entrance into the royal caves, his passage into Golden Vale had been com- paratively easy. The accusation against you of an attempt to break into liana's treasure house was a base concoction of Goya's. I swear that the guilt of the desecration of the royal dead lies upon Goya's heart alone. You jilted me for Olive, but I would not revenge myself on her. Some men care not who suffer, so long _ as they are revenged. Goya was one of them. Muro was blamed, and your wife almost sacrificed, to satisfy the villain's wounded pride." "But since, you have been in league with Muro." "It is false!" excitedly answered Lucia. "And moreover, I bring Sandy Dees as witness that I wished to avert this calamity. I warned him." "Knowing full well," I retorted savagely, for even to my shame I acknowledge that I had no faith in Lucia's pleading, "that it was impossible for me to reach the camp before the attack.' "Then why have I secreted provisions for you at your camping ground yonder ? " I demurred by simply murmuring to Mark: "As a blind,' mebbe." He shrugged his broad shoulders, and signifi- cantly muttered: "I comprehend." "You remember," continued the persistent Spaniard, "that two shots were fired while we danced together on the sward near the cascade, one of which passed through my garment. Muro can prove his innocence by means of an alibi. Some who pose as saints may be culpable." She nodded her head several times to emphasise her reply, This irritated Mark, whose white brow seemed to have written upon it: "What meanest thou, Delilah ? " Then I interposed with: "You told me you were rich. If Muro has not supplied the money, who has? "

She curled her lip with disdain, the while her eyes flashed anger. 'Mountaineer," she said spite- fully, "stick to your own trail, or you may fall among thieves,' a snub which my impudence deserved. 'Then turning to Mark, she.added more kindly: "The calm way in which my countryman i awaits your decision s a proof that he is no cowardly cur." , "Your arguments are insufficient," said Mark, "even for me to grant a reprieve." "Then at least you will concede this—that he shall be shot rather than hanged." Mark shook his head, and Lucia added: "It is the only way for tongues to be silenced about the Transatlantic past!" A visible shudder passed through Mark's body; and she continued: "Grant that I may close his eyelids and place some monument to his memory." . .

Now,' as Mark stood hesitating, like one dumb- founded, Lucia's eyes filled with tears; but she quickly brushed them away, saying: "If not for expediency's sake, still for my satisfaction, give your decision. If I i_uxrantee your safety from all danger which threat.. us Miro leaves for "Spain, never to return—if I place wealth within your grasp, will you give me Muro's life ? Remember, were the sitt ation reversed, I would plead for you." "You have given us no po itive proof of his inn), e ice," said Mark with a subdued voice, like one fully conscious of his own shortcomings. "Spare yourself further psi- , and go away. The stubuk rn knave h s refused to take food for two days; and as for speaking, not open his mouth. Still, he shall be ht, like a degraded soldier, and not hanged, as e deserves. No, no," he added, raising his hand dei.recatingly as she was about to plead further, "I will make no further concessions!" .

If a woman's hear, t is set upon a good object, she will wheedle or burrow with the industry of a beaver until she gets her way. Thus Lucia sprang a few paces, and intercepted Pepys as he was going to order the execution. 'Mark," she pleaded, in the old winsome way that sounded so familiar to our ears, "listen a moment, and you will forgive Muro." And placing her hand on his arm, she faced him, her keen eyes gazing straight into his as she whispered a sentence or two which I failed to catch, but which seemed to paralyse him. Then this sturdy man heaved a deep sigh, as if the troubles of the whole world suddenly burdened his soul. Indeed, he leant against a pine tree, so great was the shock produced by her words. Lucia put her hand gently upon his shoulder, and as I moved closer to render any necessary assistance I heard her pathetically murmur: "If you are now convinced I speak the truth, let me have his life. To many virtues, which I envy you, add one more: a noble-hearted pardon." "Sandy," was Mark's subdued order, "you and the lads must forgive me for climbing down. • . . God knows there is no help for it. . . . If you have any regard for me, don't add to my misery by asking me why. Never even refer to it. If the Senorita can nurse him back to health, let her try: I join my wife; you follow on at once. Thank God, I wipe my hands uf a strange foul stain!" And turning sorrowfully to Lucia, he added: "Senorita, I accept your conditions, and grant your request. If he recover, let him leave for Spain at once. Tell him what I say, and why I restore him to liherty."

- „ And Mark walked through the wood as one stricken with melancholia. Lucia and I joined the lads, who were naturally impatient at this delay in the ghastly business. Quick as a fawn, she sprang to Muro s side and spoke to him; but he closed his eyes like one struck with blindness, and despondently shook his head. Then she slipped the noose from his neck, and he let her lead him to the trunk of a fallen tree close handy. I followed, wondering what next she would do, when all of a sudden she turned upon me like a lynx at bay, and half-shrieked at me: "How long has he refttsed food? Am I mad, or only confused? Was it two days you said ? " "The Senorita heard correctly," I replied. "He 'had the best attention from—" Moro put up his hand and checked me naming his benefactress. This was the first intelligent movement he made, his previous acts being simply mechanical responses to the wishes of his captors. "Tell Mark Pepys," she cried in great anguish, "that his forgiveness has come too late. He knows now how easily one may misjudge. Far better had your master shot Muro outright than he should be doomed to a lingering death I" "I know nothing of the confidence that _passed between you and Mark," I replied; "but Muro's life cannot commend him to our pity. In his mad attack he spared none of us. No man, save the large-hearted Mark Pepys, would have for- given him." "His crimes were committed under a misappre.

nension, re-coma imcia wail a sneer; "ne nes escaped summary death only to endure horrible torture. Muro will die of lockjaw ! Could you wish Satan hinitelf a worse death than slow star. vation ? " The horror of this revelation struck terror into my soul. Even if it were the visitation of HeaverSi for untequited guilt, still the consciousness of a sane man in the prime of life dying inch by inch made my flesh creep. For many hours the brigand had known his doom was sealed one way or the other, and yet he had the grit to endure pain and face death. Another proof that there are points' worthy of admiration even in what men would terra the "greatest scoundrel unhung." I stood aghast, wondering whether some demon had worked the oracle. Then, with the picture of Lucia gently leading hfuro to the corral, I! turned my back upon our "location." The lads joined me, glad to forsake a place so bound npi with disagreeable memories.

Now, the strangest phase of the climax war M'ark's refusal to eat of the provisions stored by Lucia in the clump; but the rest of us were not! troubled with the qualms of conscience. Possiblyi he thought of his narrow escape from death by poison. We risked it, suffering nothing in conse- quence, and so proving Lucia's fidelity in this instance.

Some days after, Mark sent me to reconnoitre the western slopes of the Sierra, and I selected a narrow couloir for my ascent, and, picking my way across a yawning hasm, debouched upon a glad's- where a few dun-headed sparrows alone cheered the solitude. Glad of this delightful ramble among the icy buttresses, I became too venturesome while Climbing the sides of a cliff, and ' for a moment. a giddiness seized me. But long experience had evidently acdustomed my fingers and muscles to involuntarily grip with great tenacity, and happily my eye soon became keen, my nerves braced, and I could laugh at the rumbling cljsris as it rolled down some fifty feet upon the hacked and torn gully beneath. Though my intellect was apparently dazed, physical instinct saved me, and seizing the beetling crags, I mounted to the summit and scanned the glorious landscape through my glass. Away to the south was a man moving slowly down the path from Mark's Peak towards the spot in the vicinity of the eagle's eyrie ; and away to the south-west, at the end of a cai:on, I saw smoke arising, probably from Lucia's camp. Then further north Was the Yosemite watershed, and down at the base of my mighty-stronghold, and near a frozen) creek, our own party. After satisfyinr, myself that we travelled the right track I descended to the plain, where Mark came out to greet me. "Sandy," said he, "I wish you would have a chat with my wife. The silly oirl, like the Indiana generally, believes in predestinaion. You remember their chemists claimed to see through opaque sub- stances and reveal the hidden mysteries of the. inviable."

"A lot o' bosh!" I exclaimed, disdainfully. "What they term deevmation we Scotchmen calf witchcraft. Kana's astrologers were ti,'inost defied; our witches were burnt. If Olive fcraterg sich strange notions the lads will say she comes o' an uncanny stQck." "Be that as it may, Olive declares her mind i* uneasy about Muro, and what is more singular, she' fancies Lucia Diaz is playing a subtle game." "Oot, yer wife is hysterical," said I lightly, "an', like women, she has jumped to conclusions. Still, I'll see her at since.' I did so, and as she described her visions I could only conclude that there must be transmission of thought through space, or a knowledge of distant events akin to the mental drama wherein creatures of the author's imagination live, act, and spaak a. though they actually existed. I tried to joke and laugh away her hallucinations, but Olive failed to appreciate my frivolity. ' "Naething will cure her o' this mental agita— tion," I urged to Mark, "but change o' scene an'

female society. Francisco."

We must press— on to Sart

"What's the good of that ? " he irritably responded, pacing up and down, while we smoked our pipes together. "I haven't a hundred dollars to set the law in motion for the restoration of my property. We are all on the same footing now— penniless! But I'm not the one to sit down and expect ravens to feed me; the age of miracles is past. Probably I shall morally improve under these adverse circumstances, but regeneration on an empty stomach is not a genuine test, for with good' feeding come evil desires." "I like yer rough-hewn philosophy, Mark. Those who hold up their hands wi' pious horror ken naethirg o' the temptations to which the rich are exposed." "Hulloal " cried he, shading his bright brown eyes with his hand, "here are two horsemen bearing towards us from the south."

The couple turned out to be Indians who were going across country to strike the Mono trail. In reply to our questions they told us how, after we left Stony Hollow, Muro had acquired ready speech, and under cover of the darkness had gone, none knew whither. They professed to know nothing concerning Lucia's n,o7etnents, and passed • On.

"Vastly queer!" I exclaimed, staggered at thil intelligence. "There's somethino- supernatural aboot Olive. Her people were great at magic." "This news is indeed stringe, " responded Mark. "Perhaps somebody is playing us a trick. Lucia evidently takes the palm for lying. I begin to think Muro was shamming. He is the very emperor of rogues and vagabonds. While he is at large we are liable any moment to attack." "Then what was the writing on the cross found by ye in my room ? " I blurted out with some spleen. "What was Lucia's confidence, that imme- diately transformed ye into sic a simpleton as t* lat Muria gang awa' scot, free ? " "You would make a good spring-heeled Jack,* he laughingly replied. "It always amuses me to see you fume and splutter. The only thing which brings you to your senses is a toothful of whiskey. I apologise for having none. But, seriously, old chap," he added, his face firm with determination, "a wise man never repeats his folly. We must sleep with one eye open, and keep our derringers primed. Nemesis has threatened to desolate and wreck my life, but some strange monitor tells me that truth will soon prevail, and I shall conquer!" Destiny was already foreshadowed, for the unravelling of a dastardly plot came speedily, and the climar was no little surprise to Mark and me. t

(To be concluded.)

WA.BRINGTON JUMPERS AT Sr. HELENS._

Thomas iowney and Tames Parker, both of War- rington, met at the Star Inn Grounds, St. Helens on Saturday afternoon in a jumping contest for £2O aside There was a good attendance. Mr. J. Parr acted as referee. Towney was favourite. The match consisted of a French single jump. Towney went on first, but his mark was beaten by Parker by two yards. Towney then tried again, but could only land another half yard to his previous jump, with the result that Parker was declared the win- ner. This is the winner's first jump in public, It expected he will be heard of further. DECORATION FOR. LOCAL VOLUNTEERS.

The number of medals received so far by Warring- ton Volunteers is 43. A further list of awards of the volunteer medal—the seventeenth since the institution of the decoration in 1894—is shortly to be:issued from the War Office, whence it has been ascertained that the number of medals awarded up to the present time throughout the service is 20,439 representing the same number of men who have given 20 years efficient service in the volunteer force. Of these 1,831 have been earned by volun- teers belonging to Lancashire corps,the grants hav- ing beenmade in varying numbers from 831 of the first award in January, 1895, to 22 in April, 1897, the most recent award, in October last, being 38. j

CHESHIRE'S NEW DEPUTY LIEUTENANT. The " London Gezette " of Friday night contains the following :—Commission signed by the Lord Lieutenant of the County Palatine of Chester: Ralph Brocklebank. Esq., J,P., to be Deputy-Lieu- tenant, dated the 17th inst. A SERIOUS FALL.

Thomas Wateifield-,-;;-Ma.n residing in Catherine- street, was on Saturday night proceeding along the street, when he fell down and broke one of his legs. Ire was taken to the Infirmary, and is now progress- ing favourably. This is the only serious accident reported at the Infirmary during the week end.

CHURCH OF THE SACRED IIEART.-ANNUAL DANCE.

On Monday evening, a dance in connection with the above church was held in the Gymnasium, the proceeds of which were to go towards lessening the debt incurred in the building of the new schools at the back of the church. The gentlemen's commit-, tee consisting of Father Ryan, Messrs. Marshall Gibbins, Frain, Newton, Higgins, and others were chiefly responsible for the success of the ball. There were about 250 present, and a substantial sum will no doubt he realised. Dr. Liston, and Mr. John Gibbins officiated as M.G.'s, and Messrs. Barton's band played capital dance music. The decorations bad been most tastefully carried out by Mr and Mrs Marshall, and an able committee. Miss Bullian was at the head of a commiitee who were in charge of the refreshments. Dancing was kept up until 3 o'clock.

THE E‘; NI/FINER, FRIDAY, DECEMBER 3O, 1.898.

" CAPITAL " CHAT.

Mr. Henniker Heaton's Christmas linx to the British Empire—for it is Mr. Heaton who is univer- sally acclaimed as the author of the Imperial post- age scheme—is still, as he moqrnially admits, a little incomplete. Australasia, South Africa, and the West Indies are not include I in the new penny postal district. But Mr. Heaton, who is now the leading authority on this subject, confidently pre- dicts teat the whole of the empire will be admitted to the benefits of the penny rate within a few more days. He goes so far as to name Easter Mon- day as the probable date of complete inclusion. A point which is not likely to escape the vigilance of our French neighbours ie the refusal of the Post- master-General to bring Egypt within the Imperial postal district. If Mr. Heaton had had his way, the penny postage stamp would have served as the novel and delicate equivalent of a proclamation of British protectorate rights. There is a widespread, and probably well-founded, expectation that the name of the member for Canterbury will be found in the forthcoming list of New Year's Day distinc- tions.

I do not know what connoisseurs would regard as the value of the bequest of works of art and of jewellery which the deceased Baron Ferdinand de Rothschild has made to the British Museum, but it is one of the costliest which has ever been made to the nation. I have heard it said that it exceeds in value even the Wallace collection of pictures. The nation owes the donation probably to the fact that the Baron was a trus- tee of the Museum at the time of his death. As his official connection with that institu- tion has been very brief, his interest in it must have quickened rapidly,so far as its art and archaeo- logical departments were concerned. In these de- partments the Baron was a master of taste and knowledge, and he found in the Museum a new field for the exercise of both. He was on the friend- liest terms with the officers of the institution,many of whom were frequent recipients of his hospitality at Waddesdon, whose choice treasures they would doub`less survey with wonder and admiration. While the country has been pouring out its thou- sands of pounds for the Gordon College, the heroes who were wounded at Omdurman, and whom the Queen recently visited, are being already, or will shortly be. shunted out of the military hospitals to complete their cure under whatever conditions they can. Convalescent soldiers are generally moved on before they are ready for di,missal, in order to make room for other invalids worse than themselves. The poor convalescents go back to the poor homes from which they came, or to any poor acquaintance who can take them in,and to a certain extent live upon charity till they are either fit to return to the army or are dismissed from it altogether as no longer fit for service. Mr. Brad- rick gave a pledge last session that. the better treat- ment of convalescent soldiers should be taken in hand ; nothing has yet been done' and when the next session opens tile wounded of Omdurman will have been scattered abro ld in an uncured state among the slums or rural villages of the country. I understand that very strong action will be taken by way of pretest, and no better argument could be found than the treatment of General Kitchener's wounded, whose unwounded comrades were cheered to the echo not very many weeks ago. No fewer than six Acts of Parliament become fully operative on New Year's Day :—The Bene- fices Act, the Inebriates Act, the Prisons Act, the Solicitors (Ireland) Act, the Locomotives on High- ways Act, and the Vaccination Act. With regard to the last-named statute, one of its provisions— that dealing with the "conscientious objector " came into force on the passing of the Act (August 12), but the sections extending the period within which a child must be vaccinated from three to six months, directing the public vac&nator to visit the child's home when requested by the parent, and modifying the existing law in other respects with a view t 3 inducing more ready compliance with the statutory require- ment, and to mitigating the penalties for disobedience have no practical effect until January 1. Similarly, althoughit has been competent since August 2 for county and borough councils to frame bye-laws under the Locomotives Act, another week has yet to elapse before the enactment itself will come into operation. The Benefices Act, the In- ebriates Act, and the Prisons Act attracted so much attention while passing through the House of Com- mons that their purport is tolerably well known. The same cannot be said, however, of the Solicitors (Ireland) Act, a useful measure introduced by Ser- geant Hemphill in association with Mr. Maurice Healy and Mr. Carson with the object of extending to solicitors in Ireland certain privileges to which solicitors in England have long had a statutory right.

The Ivetigh bequest to the Jenner Institute will give an extraordinary impulse to original research. So handsome is the amount that the Institute can now enter upon the most extensive and patient bacteriological investigations. The result may, to some extent, redeem our national reputation,which is a long way behind the status of the Conti- nent in this region of scientific inquiry. The Institute has been hitherto very imperfectly provided with scientific apparatus,and it is rather strange that countries which envy the enormous wealth of England are so conspicuously ahead of us in everything that relates to the higher scientific progress. I hear that Dr. Nansen has almost completed the preparation of the scientific results of his journey across the Polar area. Since he finished his round of lectures in Europe and America Dr. Nansen has devoted the whole of his time to working out and preparing for publication the scientific data accu- mulated during the voyage of the Pram and on the sledge journey with Johansen. A large number of distinguished specialists have collaborated with Dr. Nansen, and it is anticipated that the publication will run to several volumes in small quarto. Such a work cannot,of course, be published as a commer- cial speculation, and I understand that the cost of its production will be borne by the " Nansen Fund for the Advancement of Science." which was largely subscribed on the return of the expedition, as a , permanent memorial of the Fram's memorable journey. As was the case with our own Challenger publications, copies will be presented to all the principal scientific societies and institutions in the civilised world. It is a tribute to the growing im- portance of English as a world language that Dr. Nansen has selected English as the medium for conveying the scientific results of his expedition to the scientific world.

Since the occasion when Sir William Harcourt denounced a letter as a forgery •in tthe House of Commons several newspapers have persistently re- frained from printing letters bearing the right hon. gentleman's signature until they have obtained his authority. The practice has its inconveniences and also its embarrassments. People have not yet for- gotten Mr. Chamberlain's annoyance when a scrupulous Press agent sent him a telegram ask- ing whether he was really the author of a particu- larly foolish attack which he had just made on Lord Rosebery. The reply was a curt "Quite authentic." Perhaps the most celebrated of the forged letters in Sir William Harcourt's name was the one in which, two or three years ago, he was made to describe Lord Rosebery as "an amiable nobleman.' The bait in that instance was too transparent for many of the newspapers, and the letter was published in only a few. Sir William was again the victim of a forgery in the course of a subsequent and somewhat heated correspondence with Mr. Chamberlain. Then confusion was worse confounded by the fact that genuine letters were mingled with the fictitious. If the ingenious cul- prit is detected, it may go bard with him. Re will have no mercy from the Press.

An inquest was held on Saturday at Penmaenmawr on the bodies ot the two men killed on the previous day by the pre- mature explosion of a charge of gelignite which was being rammed home. Dr. le Nave Foster, her Majesty's Inspector of Mines, called attention to the dangerous character of gelignite, and to the frequency of accidents arising from its flee; and the jury, in returni., g a verdict of accidental death, urged the Home Office to institute an inquiry into the suitability of the explosives in question for quarrying operations.

GENERAL NEWS.

There have been 108 deaths from plague, all at Tamatave, since the outbreak in Madagascar. An elderly married woman named Davis Was burned to death at Kettering on Saturtay night. The two or three hundred soldiers who were believed to have perished in the terrifie snowstorm in Monteue4ro have reach sd their destination in safety. The man Pearson, who committed the outrage at the British Embassy at Washington. has escaped from the lunatic asylum to which he had been sent. Two men were drowned on Monday while " larking" on the quay at Gateshead-on-Tyne. and a third, who tried to recover the bodies, had a narrow es3ape. The official residence in Washin4ton of the Chilian Minis- ter has been burned to the ground. The Minister's wife narrowly escaped perishing in the fl ernes. At Serogton, Derbyshire. on Saturday, two farmers, named Beck and Tipper, were rabbiting when the latter was killed by the accidental discharge of the former's gun. The Pope was on Suaday the recipient of a aunnerously signed address on parchment and a sum of 50,010 francs pre- sented by a number of ladies from New Yotk. Henry Spurrier. a private in the Royal Marines, was re- manded at Fareham, on Monday, on a charge of fatally stabbing Private James Whatmore, of Ihe same corps. The Swedish expedition under Dr. J. Stalling, which has been conducting a search in Northern diberia for traces of Herr Andrea, has just returned to Stockholm. The search was fruitless. A train from Ashford to Hastings collided on Monday morning with a light enema near Appledore, Kent, and eighteen persons were injured, two of them having their legs broken. A gas explosion occurred on Monday in the dressing-room of the Olympic Theatre, Wych-street, Strand. London, and Harold Haymer was so seriously injur,d that he had to be conveyed to hospital. At btechford Station on Wednesday, a ticket collector, in advancing along the platform to an in-coming trains, slipped on apiece of orange peel and fell in front of the train, being shockingly injured and killed. Sarah Jane Bainbridge and Elizabeth Armstrong, domestic servants, were remanded at Newcastle one Wednesday. on a charge of haying set fire to their employer's house and ad- ministered food containing poison to their mistress. . . _ . _

Frederick Wild, of 5, 1-Folt-Btreet. Stockport, met with a shoeking death on Tuesday at the factory of Messrs. Battersby and Ca., in that town. He was caught in tin maehinery and terribly mangled, one arm being torn oft.

John George Turner is under remand at Manchester on a charge of causing the death of Samuel Brown, living in Charlton on-dledlock, whom he is alleg d to have stabbed with a poniard in the region of the heart during a quarrel. At the North London Police Court en Tuesday, Zhigmmd Lobovitz and Isaac Stullock ware each lined 2L9a, or three months' imprisonment, for being found on the premises 93, Lewin-road, Stoke Newington. while an Illicit still was being worked.

A deaf mute named 111atthew O'llara was found dead in a neighbour's house at S.ockton-on-Tees on Sunday morning. Ile was left helplessly drunk on a couch on Christmas Rye, and must have fallen face downwards on the Itior and been suffocated.

According to " Kemp' Mercantile Gazette." the number of fahures in England and Wales gazetted during the week ending December 24th, was If. The number in the corres- ponding week of last year was 70 showing an increase of 2 being a net increase in 1893, to dat 1, of 201.

An hotel and several houses in the town of Airolo. situated at one end of the Gothard runnel, hive been destroyed by a landslip from that part of the Gothard Mountain known as the Red Rock. At least four persons have been killed. The St. Oothard Railway sustained no damage. A collision took place on Sunday morning off Whitby be tween the steamer Parborne and the schooner Racer, of In verness, the latter sinking in three minutes. The crew had a narrow escape, having only time to clamber on board the steamer before their vessel disappeared.

The death sentence recently passed at Derby Assizes on Ruth Jane Talbot for performing an illegal operation which resulted fatally, has been commuted to twelve years penal servitude, with an intimation that if prisoner's behaviour is good she will be released in eight years. A Norwich cabinetmaker named Albert Cox fired on Satur- day night at a waitress to whom he had been paying his attentions, and also at the proprietor of an eating house and his son. None or ahe shots did damage, but Cox immediately turned the weapon on himself with fatal effect. A widow named Ann Beech /It, died In the St. Helens Hospital franc burns sustained on November 17. Arm Harley, a widow, died in the Leigh Workhouse Hospital, on Monday, from burns sustained in her own house on Christ- mas morning owing to the explosion of a paraffin lamp. Tho Bridlington Guardians had before them on Saturday a letter from the solicitors of the Hon. Mrs. Monson, declining to pay £1 per week towards the maintenance of Mrs. Monson her daughter-in-law, the wire of Monson of Ardlamont noto- riety. She offered 7s. 61. per woek. The matter was ad- journed. The inhabitants of the Bemiiiis, a group of islets belonging to the Bahamas, are threatened with extermination by famine, their crops and storehouses having been destroyed by severe storms, in which many people lost their lives. Some of the famine stricken islanders have alresdy died of starvation.

A shocking fatality is reported from Cookstown, county Tyrone. A pubic house belonging to two sisters, Maria and Lizzie fil'aurk, in a mountainous district, caught fire, and the elder sister returned to the hopse to recover a large sum of money, tier sister foilowed,and the roof fell in upon them both being burnt to death. The trial of Madame Paulmier. who fired a revolver at M 011ivier, the editor of the Llnterne, last September, on account of an article which reflened upon herself arid her husband, took place before the Paris Assize Court on Mon- day. The lady was acquitted, but was ordered to pay 5,003 francs damages to M, 011ivier, who intervened as civil plaintiff.

A painful scene occurred at Grimsby on Saturday during the progress of the League match between Grimsby and Gainsborough. One of the spectators, Fred Belton, a fire- man, residing in the town, was observed to fall back unconscious, and he expired almost immediately. It is supposed that the cause of death was syncope, produced by excitement.

Mr. Henry Mandy Simmone has issued a writ against the Duke of Devonshire claiming £lO.OOO damages in respect of alleged loss of the sea view of the Alexandra Hotel and other houses in Mostyn-terrace, on the sea front at TCastbourae. The claim arises out of the erection of the William Terries Memorial Lifeboat Houvi, for which the Duke of Devonshire gave a site.

An extraordinary incident occurred at St. [James's Palace on Sunday night, when a man got past the guard, and only stopped when threatened with a loaded rifle. He declared that he wanted to see the Prince of Wales, for whom he had a letter from Jesus Christ. It was only after a severe strug- gle that eight policemen succeeded in getting him into an ambulance waggon.

A terrible tragedy has been discovered at Criccieth. For about a week nothing had been seen of a man named William Williams, or of his wife and two children, and one constable breaking into Williams's house, the whole family were found to have been ahet dead, Williams had a revolver in his hand, an it is supposed that he had murdered his wife and child' ren, and then put an end to himself. Two men quarrelled in a bedroom of a Birmingham lodging house on Saturday night, the altercation continuing for some time in the dark. Subsequently one of the pair was found lying dead on the floor. Ildward Gulch, rag and bone gatherer, was arrested on a charge of osusing his death. He stated he struck the deceased in self defence. No marks of violence are visible on the bxly of the deceased, who remains unidentified.

A miss on the one pert and a missfire on the other. and, of course, injury to neither. was the outcome of the first of the duels at Buda-Pesth on Wednesday morning, the com- batants being Biron Fejarvary, Minister of Public Defence, sad Count liarolyi. A duel with swords took place at noon between M. Ciajari and M. Szemere. In each of the first two bouts the latter was wounded in the head, and the seconds then decided that honour was satisfied.

An inquest was held ley the Monmouthshire coroner on Tuesday on some bones found at Langstone limekilns, near Newport. The deceased had evidently gone to the kilns for warmth during the night,andbeen overcome by the sulphur- ous fumes, and absolutely cremated, only a portion of the skull and part of the thigh-bone being recovered. The doctor was unable to determine the sex of the victim. This is the fifth death which has occurred at these kilns.

Fire broke oat on Monday morning in the dwelling-house, 20, Abram-street. Netherfleld-road, Liverpool, and an elderly woman named Mary Ann Burnside suciumlaed to Buff °cation and shock. Police Constable Christian bravely dashed in through the smoke and flames, and, doubtless assuming that she was still alive, handed her body to some persons through a window. , While endeavouring to re-enter the house he WAS overcome, and fell from the window to the street, a distance of twelve feet, injuring his shoulder and finger. _

On Wednesday night, Benjamin Dobson, aged sixty-eight, one of the North-Eastern Railway Company's oldest em- ployees, and latterly traffic manager at Tebay Juuetion, was killed at Tebay Junction. It is supposed that the deceased was standing en the footboard of a moving train, when, in attempting to alight, he lost his balance and fell down ths space between the station platform and the train. Several coaches passed over him, completely severing one arm and one leg from the body. He died shortly afterwards. - • -

Yet another Instance of the danger attaching to the use of the common paraffin oil lamp was givea on Saturday night In Loierpool. A married woman, named Mary Alice Capstick, living with her husband in Poplar street. Eve rton. attempted to handle a lighted oil lamp when it fell to the ground and exploded. The burning oil instantly fired the woman's garments, and ere George Loadbetter, the tenant- of the house, attracted by her screams, came to her- assistance, she was fearfully burned about the face and body. The woman was speedily removed to the Stanley Hospital, where she died on Sunday evening,

Early on Wednesday morning, a fire, resulting in three children, aged respectively ten, seven, and five. being burnt to death, occurred at Heighington, near Lincoln. Two houses were burnt down, the fire originating in that occupied by Mrs. Hammond, a widow. Upon her discovering the outbreak, she at once awakened her five children aud told them total:ow her, but, unfortunately, two became terrified and stayed behind. As the fire did not seem serious, the eldest boy, aged ten years, was sent back to fetch them whilst, the mother raised an alarm. But the house was largely hunt of wood, and soon was like a furnace. When the corpses were discovered, the youngest boy, five years of age, was found roasted to a cinder, and the eldest boy was found near the window, with his sister clasped tightly in his arms.

CHRISTMAS AT WARRING- TON.

One could not say win any degree of truth that the weather for the few days previous to Christmas Day were at all suggestive of the season. The say- ing" This is the festive season ! Let us rejoice !" seems incongruous when it is damp sad deoressing, but at the eleventh nour so to speak, just the slightest tonna of frost marked Ghe advent of Christ- mas eve. But even to the insular Englishman—con- sidered t be, ahove all things, matter of fact and void of sentiment—Clristmas time without frost loses its chief charm and characteristic fea- ture. Christmas in Warrington has been a time of general contentment, as for a con- siderable period business or trade—whichever term is most favoured by the reader—has enjoyed a course of uninterrupted smoothness. The towns- people have been well catered for in the way of amusements, and the theatre, circus an I concert hall have all rea,al 43)1 harvests. Thl fo3tball field has had some excellent' gates" and ashisual in the 4pantomitne season, Warrington folk have visited aanctiester and Liverpool in large numbers. It is a matter of satisfaction also that those who overindulge .n alcoholic refreshment at such times have been good tempered and well behave I. The fine weather which ushered in Christmas did not last for on Bank Holiday rain fall rather has vily in the morning. On Tuesday night a total eclipsej of the moonwast be observed between 8-35 p .m.,and 2-491a.m. The eclipse was not of an intense nature as at its greatest the moon remained as plainly visible as when she is looked at through :a glass rather thickly smoked, and even thelvoleanic markings could be easily distinguished. THE WORKHOUSE. _ _

Oa Christmas Eve,the children at this institution gave their annual entertainment in the schoolroom. There were the present Mayor and Mayoress, (Alderman and Mrs. Wright) the Ladies' V,siting Committee and lady visitors, Dr. and Mrs. Braide,Mr and Mrs. Brierley, and Rev. H. V. Pigot (chaplain). The programme was made up of songs, recitations, etc., and was nicely gone through by the children, who are taught by Miss Ward. Several of the children were dressed in bright colours to represent flowers, and this together with the Christmas decorations, plants, pictures, etc. made the room look very pleasant. After the concert the Chairman thanked the ladies and gentlemen who had came at this season at great inconvenience, especially the Mayor and Mayoress, who wile all the (demands noon their time still found opportunity to cmcne and see the children. A BUSY TIME AT THE POST OFFICE.

Christmas always entails additional work at the Post Office, but this year the pressure of work was unpre- cedented, and it speaks volumes for the tact and re- sourcefulness of the officials when we Say that it was carrel thro teh without delay or mistakes. Ordinarily about 200,000 missives pass through the Warrington Post Office in the coarse of A week, but during Christ- mas week this number w is increased ten-fold that is to ear about 2,003,000 were dealt with. Be- sld's those there was a great increase in the parcel post work, and it is cornnuted that about 20,000 parcels wors despatched. This is far in excess of any previous year and creates a record for the Warrington Post Offi-e. The most careful arrange- I mints had been made to successfully cope with the work, and in no detail did any hit la occur. A number of clerks from various places of business in the town woo engaged for the simoler work, and they performed their duties in a very satisfactory manner. Friday night was the heaviest night for despatch, the public having, to a larger extent than usual, acceded to the request of the P istmaater to post early. The delivery in the town on Christmas Day was very effectively carried out. In the country districts, where usually there is no Sunday delivery, special arrangements had been made, and letters were delivered as in the town, Six collections from the boxes in the town suffice for an ordinary day but hourly collections were made on Friday and Satur- day. The walks were diyidecl,and horses and carte were employed conveying letters and parcels to and from the Post Office. Additional work Naas also dealt with in consequence of the post bags from the Atlantic liners -being sent on to tVarrington by the low level line, and transferred. No 1;•ss than 100 sacks were dealt with. At the Post Office the scene on Friday and Saturday was of the liveliest description. The usual sorting room was found to be quite inadequa`e for the number engaged at the work, and a room in the basement had to be fitted up and used as an extra s -rting room. So great was the despatch that it was found necessary to engage ex,ra vehicles to take the bags to Manchester both an Friday and Saturday nights. Nearly 4,500 parcels were taken on the two nights, and as it was impossible for these to be conveyed by the mail coach, special con- veyances were engaged, and despatched specially to Manchester. The arrangements were capably directed by Mr. Viney, the Postmaster, assisted by the Chief Clerk, Mr. Broadhurst. The whole staff worked

zsalcrisly and chserfullv for lonz hours at a spell. When the great strain of work was over the men were regaled with a substanti,l meal at the net OEle on Christmas Eve, and on Cnr.strnls Day. DISTRIBUTION Al' TEE DRILL HILL.

EMBEZZLEIIENT BY A. WARRINGTON

OLE ILK.

A PAINFUL CASE.

At the Warrington Borough Pelee Court on Friday morning, before the Mayor (dldaman Wright) and other magiitrascs, Samuel Masoa, 2, Ellesmere-road. Stockton Heath, a clerk and money collector in the em- ploy of Me,srs. Williams and Clay, mormtnimtal masons, Cro3field-street,Warrin2ton was sumin-ined for embezzle- in g moneys from his employers —Mr.Firshaw appeared t) prosecute, and Mr. A. Browne, who defendei,pleaded not guilty. _

Mr. Forshaw said it was part of defendant's duty to collect money from customers, and on the 18th August he collected 19i horn Mr. McLeary of Wid- nes, and gave a receipt in the c2uclerfoil book for 53. From the same person he collected the following same : 103 on September 9'h, and ac osunted for 7s 6i ; 10s on both October 10th arid 28th, and accounted for 756.1, 011 each occasion. There was no doubt that defendant had euilmzzled sf:veral sums of money, amounting to a con- siderable sum, but complainants were only proceeding with those mill amounts. Since the proceedings had been instituted defendant wrote to Messrs. Williams and Clay to the effect that the Rev. E. T. Reed had called upon him, and told him the result of a conversation he had had with the firm. Be wa3 sorry they could not take any other course, but he begged of them, not for his own sake, but for his daughter and sons to give him another chance. Be owned his guilt, and he did not wish to make any ex- cuse, but threw himself on their mercy. Mr. Reed had told him to sign the pledge, and he earnestly intended to keep it. Be asked not to be tak-n before the magic. trates, as he could not bear it. Mr. Forshaw baid ho did not know how he could plead not guilty after that letter.

Evidence in support of the charge was given by Wats. ter Clay, George Clay, and James McLeary, Widnes. Defendant said he had worked for Messrs. Williams and Clay for more than 18 years, and his wages bad only been raised 2i. since he had been there, making them 323. per week. In August the firm stopped paying for his dinners and incidental expenses. Sometimes he would be at a loss of 25. a day, and he admitted taking the money. He collected about £4O per week from ens. tomers. His wile died a favv months ago, and his home was sold up. Mr. Browne said defendant had given his evidence very openly, and had admitted everything. He had taken the money because he had not been paid his expenses. Complainants had nothing against him before. Ths Rev. E. T. Reed spoke eloquently on behalf of the defendant and asked the magistrates not to be severe with him as his daughter was almost heartbroken. The chairman said the case wes a very painful one. Defendant would be bound over in the sum of £2O to come up for judgement if called upon withia sir months. He was also ordered to pay the costs of the case.
