THE EXAMINE,U.

DECENIBER 30th, MS.

Thursday was the birth- .. marine Ili" . White day of one of our most now er. illustrious Euglishoson—Mr W. E. Gr..tosToxr,—whose death, lying in state, and funeral are still fresh in the public memory. People are asking whether his memory is to be left to the politicians and to history, without any national effort, ceremony, emblem, or organisation to keep alive tile personal work, and long and devoted sereic.,:si the unselfish and lofty states- manship of the greatest political leader we have had since the days of PITT, it is a national ques- tion. If it has been found advisable to organise a perpetual tribute to the memory of Lord ExacoNsFrEto, who was not a typical English- man, how much !acre should we be anxious to retain in our attractions the !roue, t!io services,

:. the birthday •of the illustr.ious man who towered so much _above him ? On the other hand,, it may he said that a flower alone will never Humoctalise a public man, and that badges are not required whoa Acts of radix- *, anent ,can be arranged by the score to show what Mr. GrAnsrosn has gone and he] pd to do for the cGuntry. There is some force in this c enten- ' tion. It must be seriously considered from every point of vio.ew. Whatever is false, raeritricious, and artificial, had batter 'se kept away from the memory of a man who was mourned at his death by the whole nation and by the civilised world.. A white rose or a white chrysanthemum could not adequately ex.iress what enthusiasts feel. It could not repreont a cause or a party, except by the same kind of associations which have been ad- ventitiously grouped round the priair,,se. Yet for the young and the generations to come, how shall we honour and pr)servo the mearory of a great man, a schelar,and a st3lesman ? Hd there bean no previous organisation in existence, perhaps the ide t. of selectin afi .wer and formini a Gladstone Lea:4me would never have arisen. Tlvre Ime been a White Rose Leagn uu if 14, had hew! left alone and helped at a crisis of its existence. it would have bi:en by this time a fi mrishing end potent body. DO We need such an orgealisatiou now P If it were in exist- AL. cue it would be very helpful. Whether it be wise

I

===== 0002601_18981230_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
to Ore4to it is the doubtful matter. Perhaps our readers will give their attention to the subject, discuss it amongst themselves, and in our columns. It would be far more useful to do this than to take a pld,biscite as to the leadership and the pressing points of a:Liberal platform. If such an organisa- tion be promoted, it must not be antagonistic to any existing body, its creed must be simple, and there must be nothing in the way of Pinchbeck honours and titles connected with it. Formed on these lines, we think such an organisation may be of real and permanent service, and promote that unity of spirit which is so often lacking in the different sections into which the party is divided. Some minor organisations might very well be absorbed by it.

B )gus THE rage for sensations is inflict- Btoeraphr. ing upon us Some curious literature. When it makes no pretension to anything batter than fietion we have no real quarrel with it. We can read it or leave it alone, and it does not concern the honour or the opinior.s of individuals, Royal or otherwise. Our desire to penetrate into private life, however,is so keen that excursions are made into domains not hitherto ex- plored in the memory of the living personages themselves. We cannot wait for memoirs and diaries, or be content to read gossip which has no pretension to be authoritative or biographical. The Queen has had, on two recent occasions, the unpleasantness of finding her views descriLed in newspapers and serials. Lord ROSEBERY has come )rward to deny the authenticity of one important conversation said to have occurred between the QUEEN and himself, and now the QUEEN has ilaa to declare that there is "no truth " in a series of conversations attributed to her by a former Maid of Honour. It is lamentable that we should see the QUEEN placed in the unfortun- ate position of having to submit to rnisreprese nta- tions in peace or denying the accuracy of pub- lished accounts. It is hardly fair to publish accounts of this kind without more than ordinary care. Tney may be inexpedient and inconvenient, and contrary to good taste. In such cases they will be passed over in silence, if they are merely of a personal character, and do not impute political motives. But when it is alleged that transcripts of actual conversations are given, in- volving high matters of policy and ideas of great importance, the public have a right to expect sotnething authentic, attested by external guar- antee or internal evidence. In the present case t.te iaternal evidence was decidedly against accu- racy. The QUEEN does not talk or write in the in- flated and vague style of the revalations,as readers of her letters and her diaries and of authentic con- versations are very well able to judge. There may have been some germs of truth in the opinions expressed but they are conveyed in a style that struck every- body as most unusual and unfamiliar. It is not necessary to say more about the matter in the way of direct comment, but we are bound to protest against this tendency to exercise highly imagina- tive powers upon such subjects .s the opinions of the Sovereign on questions of State and policy. We are equally bound to protest against the general tendency to purvey bogus biography to , meet the demand for the extraordinary and the sensationaL It has become a morbid habit, and in the QUEEN'S case it is not justified by the absence of such personal and domestic details as are calcula- ted to satisfy a healthy curiosity. The QUEEN has published some of her diaries, and she lives amongst us with no Oriental desire to conceal her life from the public gaze. There might be other methods of redress open to a subject whose opinions were published in the form complained of, but for the Sovereign a simple denial must suffice, and it will be accepted without question by the public at large.

The story of M. Zola's five months sojourn anonymously in England forms a curious compli- ment to the Liglish nation. If there be one in- dividuality which has impressed itself on the French mind throughout the Dreyfus proceedings it is that of his apologist who endangered reputa- tion, property, and even liberty on his behalf. The great realist, by importing himself into the contro- versy at the moment he did, got to be more hated by a large portion of the populace than Dreyfue himself. But when he chose to leave the country and come over to England, he passed to London without recognition completely baffling the , French police who, deceived by the smallest of subterfuges—Lis wife's frequent journeys to Medan —concentrated their attention on that ' quarter. Then to London came the author : I—a Frenchman knowing not a word of English alone, and betraying by his every action the features and figure then being illustrated in every other newspaper of the coun- try. He made his way, however, to an obscure village on the Birmingham line, and there pre- served his secret. It is true that Ins identify was , suspected very strongly by the local clergymen, who for that reason, with a File old English courtesy, paid him marked attention without ask- ing any questions. But there, and in the other places to which he has moved during his five months' stay in this country, no attempt was made to unravel the mystery. Even the newspapers, which claim now to have superseded the detectives —at least in America—continued to state oracu- larly that Z.ila was in London, when he was not there. The novelist thinks all this was done in kindness, and that the good people veiled their eyes, s to speak,to 1-is recognition. That is a very pretty compliment to the density of the English people ; as largo a one as it is possible to pay.

Duelling never has .quite died oat, except in this country, and on the Continent lethal weapons are still resorted to with some frequency for the settling of disputes. "Coffee and pistols for two." is a programme of some popularity in Hun- gary at present, and scarcely a day passes but a couple of irate nobleman or statesmen try con- clusions with one another by means of the driello. We read of fierce encounters, feverish excitement, doctors in anxious attendance, and no result but a trilling scratch or an entirely harmless burning of powder. Seeing that the opponents are ustm Ily rso bitter against each other be- fore the affray it is a little remarkable that they hould he so careful to avoid inflicting any injury. Anything more farcical and childish than a French or Hungdrian duel we can- not imagine, Yet there is ono thing to be said in favour IS these affaires d'houneur. They suffice to tv.suage the wounded feelings of the dispiitants at a very small test either monetary or physical.

Stti.:factory progress we reported at the annual maetitig of the Warrington Savings Bank last week. If then:unbar of business transactions may be tlikan as a reflex of the state of prosperity in the town, thou they are very encouraging. Uu doubtedly Warrington has enjoyed a period of good trade during the past twtilve months' miin- terrupted by disputes of a serious kind, and it is pleasing to note that there is every prospect of its continuance in the New Year. In fact at the pre- sent time the iron and allied trades seem to be in a healthier condition than has been the case for sonic time past. It is therefore advantageous that there should he such :in excellent local institution avi the Savings B o:lc wher. s working- classes can d,.josr their Small savings with absolute se anti y awl he the better preplred for the p: ,vermal "raiuy day." THE EX ./k MINER FRIDAY. D 1 CEMBER 30, 1.-t-)8 RUNCORN AND WIDNES. !CHRISTMAS IN RUNCORN.

by the number of small accounts, 4,794 being of one pound and under. During the year 928 fresh accounts we e entered, and it is to be hoped that when the next report is presented a still greater addition will have been made to the number of those who are endeavouring to be thrifty.

The princely munificence of Lord Iveagh has been the principal topic of conversation since Friday; and well might it be so for a gift of half a million pounds sterling is not an everyday event, or at all a "frequent occurrence. The noble lord has been impressed with the deplorable state of Slumdom in Dublin, or at any rate that part of it lying between the two cathedrals, and to ameliorate the condition of things he proposes to expend a round sum of £250,000 in clearing the land of its wretched tene- ments, and covering the area wjth workmen's dwel- lings, model lodging houses, a library, concert hall, swimming bath, gymnasium, and recreation ground. Ineffectual attempts have been made by the Dublin Corporation on several occasions to do away with these slums—perhaps the most miser- able of any to be found in the kingdom—but the task has proved one of almost unsurrnountable difficulty to the city governors. Now Lord Iveagh proposes to solve the problem himself by Private Bill powers, and the Corporation of Dublin ought 1 to be, and no doubt are, deeply grateful to him. The property to be erected on the cleared and cleansed area is to be afterwards invested in the Guinness Trust, and the income which will be de- rived should make the improvement a lasting one. We could find one or two equally deserving slums in Warrington, if the millionaire is forth- coming who will be prepared to follow the noble example of Lord Iveagh. It is to be feared, however, that such good fortune as Dublin is about to experience will never be the lot of War- rington. The remaining £250,000 of the half- million will benefit mankind all the world over. This sum is plaoed in the hands of the Jenner Institute for the endowment of research directed to the prevention of disease. Scarcity of funds has always been the great drawback of scien- tists endeavouring to successfully resist the ravages of plague and contagious disease, and the gift of Lord Iveagh should go far towards making their labours really effective.

ReBBERrEN AT BANE QUAY STATION —William Baker was charged with stealing from a brake van at Bank Quay Station last night a blue pilot overcoat the property ef the London and North Western Railway Company.—Detective Howell said there was other property recovered by the police after the defendant's arrest and be would therefore ask for a re- mand for a week to investigate the matter. Prisoner was employed by the Company as a lamp lighten—Detective Roper gave evidence of the arre‘t of prisoner at 4 o'clock on Thursday.—The bench granted a remand for a week. "DRUMMED FROM THE AMMY."— Jain William Travis was ``.

charged on a warrant wth committing damag to windows on December 20th to the amount of 7s.—Defendant pleaded guilty. -Mrs Travis, prisoner's mother said he broke the win dows to get in her house —The Chief Constable said it was a very bad case indeed Prisoner had been 12 times before the magistrates for assaulting his fathe- and mother and stealing their goods from the house etc. Prisoner had been in two or three regiments and been drummed out of them all The Bench committed him to gaol for one month with hard labour. - -

AN UNCLEANLY SLAUGHTER HOlTSE.—Walter Shaw, butcher, Orfcrd-tane, was suoarnonei far breaches of the Warrington bye-laws, by unlawfully failing to wash the - Walls of the slaughter house, and also with neglecting to remove certain refuse within the required time —lnspector Borman stated that the defendant kept premises at 104, Orford-'sne. Since the defendant had been the owner he , had frequently had cause to complain about its filthy and neglected condition. The premises were visited by the Melt ca 1 Officer and Mr. Powell. The walls were splashed with blood, and had not been washed, and the refuse had not been removed. The detendaat said, he • was the owner of the slaughter house but did not nee it -He sub-let it to ethers, and they neglected to keep it clean. • He had decided to give it up altogether.—Dr. ornall said , they bed had great difficulty with the defendant, and with their somewhat small staff it was quite impossible to keep a survey over every possible nuisance in Warrington. It was a pure accident that he visited the house on Tuesday.—The Bench imposed a fine of lOs and costs in each case, 32s in all. AN UNHAPPY MARRIAGE.—John Cullan. 14, Blue Coat- street, was summ.ned on a charge of assaulting his wife Emma Cullen on the 12th lost —The complainant, who had been married to the defendant for only nine months stated that on the date named her husband came home drunk and asked her to get him a pair of clogs. She toll him she was unable to do so, and he then got hold of her by the neck and struck her. She went out as she was frplatened of him, and when she returned the following day he struck her and put her on the floor with her chili.—The defeudant denied striking her. He was bound over to keep the peace for six months in his own recognizanees of £5, and ordered to pay the costs.

The congregation signified their approval by standing in a body. St. MARY'S CATHOLIC CHURCH.

Christmaa -Day at St. Mary's Catholic Church, Butter- market-street, was celebrated with much solemnity. Each priest offered up three Masqei, this being the only day in the year upon which he can do so. At half-past six the first Mass commenced,and at every half hour up to ten o'clock Masses were said, each low Mass occupy- ing half an hour. At eleven o'clock the grandest cere- mony of the Catholic Churth began, namely, Solemn Hjh MARS. The celebrants of the different Masses were the Superior of St. Mary's (Father N.V. Wilson, 0.5.8.), Father R.M. Lucan, 0.5.8., and Dr. C. P. Mercer,O.S.B. At all the early Mass.s people ammoached the altar rails to receive Holy,Communion. During the offertory at the &even o'clock Maas, the Christmas hymn " Adeste Filelis "was sung. The church was most tastefully de- corated, the windows and the canopies of the stations of the cross being filled with evergreens. A representation of the birth of the Saviour at Bethlehem proved a strong attraction to thc congregation.

VERY LIKELY.—A countryman sowing seed was accosted by two would•be smart fellows on horse- - back, and one remarked with an insolent air, "Well, honest fellow, it is your business to sow, but we reap the fruits of your labours." UNREASONABLE.—They were organising a land company, when Jobson said to Hobson: "We've got everything ready. I've got an artist to paint a picture of the land as it is, and another as it will be." "But where is the land? Have you bought it yet ? " "Bought it? Why, how can we buy it until we have sold our stock?" responded Jobson, who prided himself on being a financier. "You're unreasonable. It takes money to buy land, you know.'
